      "raw_content": " [Skip to content](#content)\n\n[![The Tuscan Mom](https://thetuscanmom.com/wp-content/uploads/2021/03/logod.webp)](https://thetuscanmom.com/)\n\n[![The Tuscan Mom](https://thetuscanmom.com/wp-content/uploads/2021/03/logod.webp)](https://thetuscanmom.com/)\n\n![Deli section in an Italian grocery store. You can see cured meats on shelves and salads.](https://thetuscanmom.com/wp-content/uploads/2022/07/coop-grocery-store-deli-italy-1024x768.webp)\n\n# ITALIAN SUPERMARKETS – A Visitor’s Guide + Helpful Tips\n\nBy   [Candice Criscione](https://thetuscanmom.com/about-me/ \"View all posts by Candice Criscione\")   /  July 30, 2022\n\n*Italian Supermarkets– different types of supermarkets in Italy, important things to know about shopping, helpful tips, useful words and phrases for shopping at an Italian supermarket and more!*\n\nAh, grocery shopping in Italy.  Is it really romantic like they show it in the films?  Skipping along cobblestone streets, stopping in at the jolly bakery and choosing the freshest produce from the friendly neighborhood fruit seller?\n\nWell, sure, some people here in Italy do still shop like that, but most of us with families and busy lives make our way to the supermarket, just like you do!\n\nHowever, there are some differences between Italian supermarkets and those in your home country.  Something as simple as buying food can be bewildering here in *bella Italia*.\n\nEven if you don’t *need* anything at an Italian supermarket, [if it’s your first visit to Italy](https://mominitaly.com/italy-travel-tips/), it’s a fun way for everyone – from [toddlers](https://mominitaly.com/italy-with-a-baby-or-toddler/) to [teens](https://mominitaly.com/italy-with-teens/) to adults – to soak up a little Italian culture.  You can learn a lot about a place and its people by exploring its supermarkets.\n\nSo, let’s take a look at Italian supermarkets and some of their unique traits.\n\nTable of Contents\n\n## **Types of Supermarkets in Italy**\n\nYou may be wondering what supermarkets and grocery stores are called in Italy.  The simple answer is: *supermercato*.\n\nIf you’re visiting Italy with your family and you need to pick up some fresh produce, [snacks](https://thetuscanmom.com/where-to-get-a-snack-with-kids-in-florence/), picnic supplies, toiletries, or even [diapers](https://mominitaly.com/diapers-in-italy/), you’ll want to ask someone where a supermarket is.\n\nThere are a few different types of grocery stores, and its helpful to know the differences between them. Italy has:\n\n* *ipermercati* (hypermarkets)\n* *supermercati* (supermarkets)\n* *convenience mercati* (smaller supermarkets in city centers)\n* *alimentari* (grocers)\n* *discount* (discount chains)\n* specialty shops\n* *mercati* (open-air markets)\n\n### *Ipermercati* (Italian Hypermarkets)\n\nThese chains are similar to a Walmart or Target in that they have food and a whole lot more!  Some of the popular hypermarkets you’ll see in Italy are:\n\n* Auchan\n* Bennet\n* Carrefour\n* Coop\n* Conad\n* Decò\n* Despar\n* Panorama\n* Sisa\n\nThey’re so large that you won’t find them in city centers – they’re usually in a periphery zone.  They always have large parking lots and are often accessible by bus.\n\nIf you’re visiting, it’s not worth going out of your way to visit one, but if you run across one, stop in to see the vast amount of Italian food and goods for sale. If you’re staying in one place for awhile and want to ‘settle in,’ this is where you’ll find the best prices on many food products, household goods, cleaning supplies, and baby products.\n\n### *Supermercati* (Italian Supermarkets)\n\nYou’ll find *supermercati* in busy cities, smaller towns, and in the countryside.  It’s the ‘regular-size’ grocery store that carries an abundant selection of fresh food, non-perishable food, household essentials (kitchen supplies, cleaning supplies, and toiletries).\n\n**Good To Know:**  These regular grocery stores are much smaller than what you’re used to at home! The aisles are smaller and there aren’t 20 options for each product (except for the entire aisles of pasta, tomato sauce, and breakfast cookies). Whenever we head back to the US I’m amazed at how spacious Safeway and other US supermarkets are!\n\nYou may also find small sections with seasonal products (like candy for Valentine’s Day, *coriandoli* for Carnevale, Easter cakes, or *panettone* for Christmas) and clothing.\n\nSome popular supermarkets you’ll see in Italy:\n\n* Carrefour\n* Conad\n* Coop\n* Crai\n* Despar\n* Esselunga\n* Pam\n* Sigma\n* Sisa\n\nWe also have specialty supermarkets like [Eataly](https://mominitaly.com/how-many-eatalys-are-in-italy/), which carry typical Italian products, including some from the local area. They’re mostly in larger Italian cities but we even have one at an Autogrill, off the Autostrada!\n\n### *Convenience Mercati* (Smaller Supermarkets in Italian Town Centers)\n\nThe Italian word for these is actually the English ‘convenience.’  You may also hear them called ‘mini-markets.’\n\nThese ‘mini-size’ big chain grocery stores are popular in city centers.  You’ll find the basics you’ll need as a tourist and locals can ‘get by’ with the food on offer, but there’s not a huge selection and the prices are usually a little higher than regular supermarkets or hypermarkets.  Most locals will also head to a regular supermarket at some time during the week or month.\n\nOften these minimarkets have ‘city’ in their names:  Conad City, for example.\n\n### *Alimentari* (Grocers)\n\nYou’ll find *alimentari* throughout Italy, often family-run with a little Italian man behind the counter reading his newspaper.  There may be an eclectic selection, and you may find dust on some of the products.  But, chances are, if you ask, they’ll have what you need!\n\nThese are often great places to gather goods for a picnic (cheese, bread, jams, olives, wine) because they get fresh products from local producers.\n\n### *Discount* (Discount Supermarkets)\n\nDiscount supermarkets have become incredibly popular in Italy.  You’ll find discounted food, fresh and non-perishable, but there’s usually less selection.    \n  \nSome have theme weeks when you’ll find foods from certain regions of Italy or Europe. Some also have theme weeks for holidays and celebrations, like [Valentine’s Day](https://mominitaly.com/valentines-day-in-italy/), [Carnevale](https://mominitaly.com/italian-carnival/), [Festa della Donna](https://mominitaly.com/festa-della-donna/), Easter, Halloween, [Thanksgiving](https://mominitaly.com/italian-thanksgiving-traditions/), Christmas, and New Year’s.    \n  \nThe biggest discount chains in Italy are:\n\n* Eurospin\n* MD\n* Lidl\n* Tuodì\n* Penny Market\n* In’s Mercato\n* DPiù\n\n### Specialty Shops\n\nThese are the beating heart of the Italian food scene.  If you’re visiting, make a point of checking out the specialty shops for the cream of the crop – the best bread, fruits and vegetables, meats, and cheeses are all for sale in these specialty shops.\n\nHead to the individual shops to get ingredients for a picnic or supplies for cooking a meal in your apartment.\n\n* *forno* (bakery)\n* *fruttivendolo* (fruit & vegetable shop)\n* *macelleria* (butcher)\n* *pasticceria* (pastry shop)\n* *pescheria* (fish shop)\n\n### *Mercati* (Open-Air Food Markets)\n\nAnother way to find the freshest, in-season food – head to the village or city open-air market.  Some are held daily, and others are once-a-week.\n\nVendors set up stands, tents, or sell out of their food trucks.\n\nYou can sometimes sample the products.\n\nObserve which stands the locals head to – they know where the best prices and best *cibo* (food) is!\n\n**In this article, I’ll be focusing on the classic Italian *supermercato* (supermarket).**\n\n## **Main Sections of an Italian Supermarket**\n\nIn an Italian grocery store, you’ll typically find the following areas:\n\n**Fresh Produce** – fresh, in-season fruits and vegetables\n\n**Bakery** – freshly baked bread and pastries\n\n**Deli** – cheeses, cured meats\n\n**Refrigerated Section** – milk, yogurt, packaged cheeses and meats, fresh pasta\n\n**Breakfast** – packaged Italian breakfast cookies and pastries, cereal, granola bars, baking ingredients\n\n**Snacks** – packaged bread and crackers, chips, nuts, juice, *merendine* (sweet and savory packaged snacks)\n\n**Fish** – fresh fish\n\n**Meat** – packaged or from the butcher: *pollo* (chicken), *manzo* (beef), *vitello* (veal), *tacchino* (turkey) and *maiale* (pork). You’ll also found ground meats (*macinato*), typically beef and pork, and sometimes turkey.\n\n**Senza Glutine** – gluten-free pasta, packaged bread, crackers, cookies. Schar is the main company for gluten-free products in Italy, but many major Italian supermarket chains now make their own gluten-free products\n\n**Organic** – ‘health food’ section with oats, non-wheat flours, healthy snacks, non-dairy milks, and more. Common non-dairy milks include *avena* (oat), *cocco* (coconut), *riso* (rice), *soia* (soy), and *mandorla* (almond).\n\n**Beverages** – large selection of still (*naturale*) and sparkling (*frizzante*) water, soda (usually Coca Cola, Coke Zero, Fanta, Sprite, San Pellegrino and a few others)\n\n**Basics** – pasta, tomato products, canned and Tetra-pak goods (*scattole*)\n\n**Toiletries** – typical toiletries like shampoo, conditioner, soap, toothbrushes and toothpaste, and first-aid items, deoderant, feminine supplies (tampons, pads, panty liners)\n\n**Household items** – toilet paper, paper towels, etc\n\n**[Baby products](https://mominitaly.com/italy-packing-list-for-a-baby-or-toddler/)** – baby food, diapers, diaper cream, baby wipes, formula\n\n**Frozen** – vegetables, meals, desserts, and other frozen foods (*surgelati*)\n\n**Alcohol** – wine, beer, cocktail ingredients\n\n**Pharmacy** – many Italian grocery stores now have mini pharmacies where you can buy over-the-counter products – a wide range of products like contact lense solution, high-end face creams, pregnancy tests, and after-bite (mosquito) ointments\n\n## **Important Things to Know About Italian Supermarkets**\n\n### You Need To Pay To Use A Cart\n\n![Small child sits in grocery cart in an aisle at an Italian grocery store.  You can see food on the shelves on both sides and in front of him - beer, rice, sunflower oil, and Kinder Egg products.  He is wearing grey sweatpants and a blue coat and is looking away from the camera.  ](data:image/svg+xml,%3Csvg%20xmlns='http://www.w3.org/2000/svg'%20viewBox='0%200%200%200'%3E%3C/svg%3E)\n\nYou can’t use a cart like this in Italy for free\n\nPut a coin in (usually €0.50 or €1 but will be written on the cart) to release the chain.   When you’re finished shopping, return the cart where you got it, put the chain back in, and release your coin.\n\nWhy do we do this in Italy?  It may seem silly, but at least we don’t end up with carts all over the lot and town.  Everyone wants his coin back, so carts get returned!\n\nIf you don’t need a cart, you can use a small (often rolling) hand basket for free.  They’re usually stacked at the store entrance.\n\n![View of small rolling hand basket at an Italian grocery store.  It's in the nut aisle - you can see peanuts and hazelnuts on the shelf.](data:image/svg+xml,%3Csvg%20xmlns='http://www.w3.org/2000/svg'%20viewBox='0%200%200%200'%3E%3C/svg%3E)\n\nUse a small hand basket for free\n\n### Use a Glove To Handle Produce\n\n*Before* you start gathering your fresh fruit and vegetables, you’ll need to put on a plastic glove.  Yes, that’s right!  ***Hygiene scorecard:  Italy 1 – Everywhere Else 0***.\n\nThere’s a dispenser (or a few) with the plastic bags in the produce area.\n\n**Good To Know:**  The plastic bags and gloves are biodegradable in many of the larger Italian grocery stores.\n\nOnce you’re gloved, feel free to touch and examine the produce.  But don’t just throw it in your cart!  You need to weigh your fruits and vegetables at Italian grocery stores.\n\n### Weigh Your Produce\n\nOnce you’ve chosen your produce, put it in a bag and note the number of the product on the sign nearby.\n\nThen, take your bagged fruits or vegetables to one of the scales (a *bilancia*) in the area.  Put the produce on the scale and enter the product number (sometimes there are pictures of the products).\n\nTake the little sticker that comes out and stick it to the bag – all done!\n\n**Good To Know:**  You can skip the bag if you’d prefer not to use one.  Just put the sticker directly on the produce.\n\n**Don’t forget to weigh your produce** – the cashier doesn’t have a scale. If you forget, you’ll need to leave the line (with everyone waiting behind you!) to go weigh it.\n\n**Good To Know:**  There are exceptions to weighing fresh produce. You don’t need to weigh it if it already has a barcode on it (for example, the lettuce on the left in the photo above).\n\n### Order Fresh Meats and Cheeses From The Deli\n\nYou’ll see packaged cheeses and meats in the refrigerated section.  They’re fine to eat, but the best-tasting cheeses and meats are in the grocery store deli.\n\nYou usually need to get a number from the machine and wait to be called (or have your number show up on the digital display).\n\nGood To Know: We order our meats and cheeses by the *etto*, or 1/10 of a kilogram (100 g).  If you need to order more, you order *etti*.\n\nFor example:\n\n*Vorrei*… (I would like…)\n\n| **Italian Weight Measurement** | **English Translation** | Weight in pounds (lbs) |\n| --- | --- | --- |\n| *un etto* | 100 grams | .22 lbs |\n| *due etti* | 200 grams | .44 lbs |\n| *tre etti* | 300 grams | .66 lbs |\n| *quattro etti* | 400 grams | .88 lbs |\n| *mezzo kilo* | ½ kilogram | 1.1 lbs |\n| *sei etti* | 600 grams | 1.33 lbs |\n| *sette etti* | 700 grams | 1.54 lbs |\n| *otto etti* | 800 grams | 1.77 lbs |\n| *nove etti* | 900 grams | 1.98 lbs |\n| *un kilo* | 1 kilogram | 2.2 lbs |\n\nListen to the pronunciation here:\n\n**Good To Know:** Ordering deli meat to make your own sandwiches? A good estimate is about 90 grams per sandwich, and I usually round up. So, if you want to make 3 sandwiches: 3 x 90 grams = 270 grams, and round up to 300 grams, or *tre etti*.  \n  \nYou can also ask the person serving you *‘per tre persone’* (three sandwiches) and he or she will slice a typical amount for three servings.  \n  \nYou can also say *‘va bene così, grazie’* or *‘basta così, grazie’* when you’re satisfied with the amount sliced.\n\n### Bring Your Own Bags Or Pay For Them\n\nIf you need to purchase bags, they’re usually €0.05-0.10 each, and are often the flimsy biodegradable bags.  At larger stores you can purchase collapsible cardboard boxes, insulated bags (for cold food), and reusable bags.\n\nThe cashier may ask if you want bags, or you may see them where you put your groceries on the scanner belt.  If you grab some there, put them on the scanner belt so the cashier can ring them up.  \n  \nIf you can, bring your own bags.  It will save you some cash and will help our planet!\n\n### Bag Your Own Groceries\n\nWhen it’s your turn to check out, place all of your groceries on the belt (or near the cashier if there’s no belt), then walk past the cashier to begin bagging your groceries as she rings them up.\n\n**Keep Things Moving:**  When the cashier has finished ringing up your groceries, stop bagging and pay.  When you’re finished paying, you can continue bagging and the cashier can begin ringing up the next customer.  There is usually a small divider in the bagging area and he/she’ll send the next customer’s groceries to the other side of the bagging area.\n\n### Use Your Receipt To Exit The Self-Checkout Area\n\nIf you choose the self-checkout area, don’t tuck your receipt away when you’re finished.  You’ll need to scan your receipt to leave the area.  Put the barcode of the receipt under the scanner and the gate will open up.\n\n**Good To Know:** The self-checkout attendant may also ask to see your receipt to re-scan your items.  It’s a random check and after the quick re-scan, you’ll be on your way.\n\n## **Other Helpful Things To Know**\n\n### Eggs Are Not Always Refrigerated\n\n### Not All Milk Is Refrigerated\n\nItaly has fresh milk, as well as UHT (ultra high temperature) pasteurized milk, which is shelf-stable.   \n  \nIf you buy fresh milk in the refrigerated section, pay attention to the expiration date – it’s much sooner than you’re probably used to seeing in your home country.\n\n### Tell The Cashier How You’re Paying\n\nIf you’re paying with a credit card, tell the cashier, and he/she will tell you when to insert your card or tap it to the machine.\n\n**Good To Know:**  While large grocery stores in Italy will accept credit cards, smaller stores often do not (even though they’re legally required to), so it’s a good idea to always have some cash on hand.\n\n### Grocery Scanners Are Only For Store Loyalty Club Members\n\nYou may see small hand scanners on display at the store entrance.  You can use them to scan your groceries and then scan the device at self-checkout to pay without having to remove your groceries from the cart.\n\nIf you want to use one, you need to be a store member and have a *tessera* (loyalty card). To free a hand scanner from the display, you scan your *tessera*.\n\n### Italian Grocery Stores Carry Toiletries\n\nGrocery stores are some of the best places to buy shampoo, toothpaste, deodorant, baby products (diapers, diaper cream, baby wipes) and other essentials.  In some towns, it may be your only option.  In larger towns, it may be your cheapest option.\n\nToiletry stores like Caddy are popping up throughout the country, and there are perfume shops as well, but many (including our family) buy toiletries at the grocery store.  We don’t have any stores with great prices like Target or Boots – yet.\n\n### Foreign Products Are Expensive\n\nYou may be missing favorite food products like peanut butter or tahini.  Don’t be surprised by high prices for these often-imported goods.  Try to eat local, seasonal foods if you can!\n\nAlso, these products may only be available during tourist season.\n\n### Italian Supermarkets Aren’t Open 24 Hours a Day\n\nItalian supermarkets are **generally open throughout the day, from morning until early evening**. Hours vary depending on the chain and where it’s located. Grocery stores in cities are typically open early and close later than those in the countryside or smaller villages, which may also close for a lunch break (typically 1:00 pm – 4:00 pm).\n\nIt’s a good idea to check the hours by calling the supermarket (or have your hotel reception call for you). Google Maps will give you a good idea of the hours but closing days aren’t always updated, so don’t rely on the information.\n\nGrocery stores in Italy **may or may not be open on Sunday**. It used to be rare to find supermarkets open on Sunday in Italy, but more and more are staying open, at least until lunchtime. Don’t rely on finding one open – always get what you need by Saturday evening.\n\n**Good To Know:**  The Autogrill (a restaurant/cafè/minimarket on Italian toll roads) is always open, so if you *really* need something, you can drive to the Autostrada and shop at the Autogrill. It’s more expensive, but you can get what you need.\n\n### Italian Grocery Stores Can Get Very Crowded\n\nItalian grocery stores (except for the large hypermarkets) are often in compact spaces with narrow aisles.\n\nIf you can, try to shop at quieter times of the day – your shopping experience will be much more pleasant if you don’t have to fight your way through hordes of people or elbow *nonne* (Italian grannies) in the long lines at the cashier (just kidding – please don’t elbow any *nonne*).\n\n## **Useful Words & Phrases For Italian Grocery Stores**\n\n### Greetings & Basic Phrases\n\n|  |  |\n| --- | --- |\n| *buongiorno/buonasera* | good morning / good afternoon or evening |\n| *arrivederci* | goodbye |\n| *per favore* | please |\n| *grazie* | thank you |\n| *si* | yes |\n| *no* | no |\n\nListen to them here:\n\n### Navigating The Italian Grocery Store\n\n|  |  |\n| --- | --- |\n| *Dov’e’…?* | Where Is…? |\n| *Scusi* | Excuse me (to get someone’s attention) |\n| *Quanto costa?* | How much does it cost? |\n| *sconto* | discount |\n| *pesare* | to weigh |\n| *Vorrei…* | I would like… |\n\nListen to them here:\n\n### At Checkout\n\n|  |  |\n| --- | --- |\n| *busta/sacchetto* | bag |\n| *carta di credito* | credit card |\n| *contanti* | cash |\n| *tessera* | loyalty card |\n| *spiccioli* | coins |\n| *banconote* | banknotes |\n\nListen to them here:\n\n### Numbers\n\n| **Number** | **Italian** | **Pronunciation** |\n| --- | --- | --- |\n| 0 | *zero* | **TSEH**-roh |\n| 1 | *uno* | **OO**-noh |\n| 2 | *due* | **DOO**-eh |\n| 3 | *tre* | treh |\n| 4 | *quattro* | **KWAH**-troh |\n| 5 | *cinque* | **CHEEN**-kweh |\n| 6 | *sei* | say |\n| 7 | *sette* | **SEHT**-teh |\n| 8 | *otto* | **OH**-toh |\n| 9 | *nove* | **NO**-veh |\n| 10 | *dieci* | dee-**EH**-chee |\n\nListen to them here:\n\n## **Staples To Buy At Italian Grocery Stores**\n\n*Olio d’oliva* (olive oil) – Best purchased after the harvest in November.  Italian grocery stores have great olive oil – you don’t need to buy expensive oil or oil from a specialty shop.\n\n*Aceto Balsamico* (balsamic vinegar)\n\n*Pasta* – You’ll find an entire aisle of it at an Italian supermarket!  In addition to the dried pasta aisle, there is fresh egg pasta in the refrigerated section. Tired of pasta? You’ll also find *riso* (rice).\n\n*Pane* (bread) – Most Italian grocery stores have their own *forno*.  We buy *schiacciata* and bread at the grocery store and in town at our bakery.  Other bread staples like *[fette biscottate](https://mominitaly.com/fette-biscottate/)* can be found in the packaged food section.\n\n*Aglio* (garlic)\n\n*Pomodori* (tomatoes) – Fresh and preserved tomatoes are staples in any Italian kitchen.\n\n*Erbe* (herbs) – *Basilico* (basil), *rosmarino* (rosemary), *timo* (thyme), *origano* (oregano), *prezzemolo* (parsley) and *alloro* (bay leaves) are used often in Italian recipes.\n\n*Sott’olio* (literally ‘under oil’) – These items, like sundried tomatoes, roasted eggplants, and olives, can be found preserved in oil in jars or in the deli. They’re great options for *[aperitivo](https://mominitaly.com/aperitivo-italian-aperitif/)*!\n\n*Sott’aceto* (literally ‘under vinegar’) – Capers, onions, and pickles are a few types of *sott’aceti* that you’ll find in jars or in the deli.\n\n*Salumi* (cured pork) – *Prosciutto cotto*, *prosciutto crudo*, and *salame*, to name a few. Buy them packaged if you’re in a hurry; otherwise, take a number and wait for your turn to order freshly-sliced meats.\n\n*Formaggio* (cheese) – Take your choice of Italy’s fresh and aged cheeses in the deli section or pre-packed in the refrigerated section. Grab classics like *parmigiano*, *gorgonzola*, or *pecorino*. Or, try a lesser-known local slice.\n\n*Frutta* (fruit) – Most Italian supermarkets follow the seasons, so you’ll find fresh, in-season produce. Look for *fragole* (strawberries), *pere* (pears), *albicocche* (apricots), *mele* (apples), *uva* (grapes), *pesche* (peaches), *arance* (oranges), *pompelmo* (grapefruit). You’ll also find non-local produce like *ananas* (pineapple), *mango*, *avocado*.\n\n***Verdure*** (vegetables) – As with fruit, you’ll find fresh, in-season veggies in Italian grocery stores, including *zucchine* (zucchini), *lattuga* (lettuce), *cetrioli* (cucumbers), *asparagi* (asparagus), *peperoni* (bell peppers), *carote* (carrots), *melanzane* (eggplants, aubergines), *cipolle* (onions), *patate* (potatoes).\n\n## **Supermarkets in Italy FAQ**\n\n**Can I pay with Apple Pay in Italy?**In most cities, Apple Pay is widely accepted, but it’s still not used much in smaller towns and in the countryside.  Make sure you have a backup method of payment with you.\n\n**Does Italy have a store like Costco?**Unfortunately, no.  At one point Costco was looking into opening a store in Italy but the project was abandoned.  While there was no official statement, I’m guessing it was a combination of (1) Italian bureaucracy, (2) the cost of doing business in Italy, and (3) the fact that Italians do not buy in bulk (lack of space in homes and avoidance of one-time large costs).  We have some bulk warehouses, but they cater more to restaurants.\n\n**Do they have grocery stores in Italy?**Yes!  Although many imagine that we shop at individual food stores (the butcher, the bakery, the fruit stand, etc), most Italians make their way into the grocery store on a regular basis.  True, the food may not be quite as high quality as at the individual vendors, but the prices and convenience make Italian grocery stores popular here.\n\n**Does Italy have grocery delivery?**Grocery Delivery boomed during the COVID pandemic and it’s still going strong.\n\nMuch like in other places, it’s not perfect.  You sometimes get a substitute or the product you want isn’t available.  And, not everything is offered for delivery.  But, for basics it’s a great option.\n\nIf you know you want to get your groceries delivered, put your order in as soon as you can, because the delivery calendar can fill up quickly, especially in cities.\n\nWe’ve used grocery delivery a few times but I prefer to choose the produce myself and I like to have the large assortment offered in the store.\n\n**Where can I find a supermarket in Italy?**Supermarkets are found throughout the country – in cities, the countryside, and everywhere inbetween. If you’re visiting a supermarket in the countryside, you’ll be able to park in a parking lot. In cities, it’s easiest to arrive by foot or public transport (even though they sometimes have tiny parking lots).\n\nIf you need to find a supermarket, you need to know the word for it and how to pronounce it! *Supermercato*, the general term for a grocery store or supermarket in Italy is pronounced **soo-pehr-mehr-KAH-toe**.\n\nYou can ask your hotel concierge, taxi driver, or a stranger on the street, *“Per favore, dov’è un supermercato?”* or *“C’è un supermercato in zona?”* (“Can you please tell me where a supermarket is?” or “Is there a supermarket in the area?”)\n\n**What are the most popular supermarkets in Italy?**The most popular supermarkets in Italy, by number of stores are:\n\n* Conad\n* Crai\n* Coop\n* Sisa\n* Despar\n* Carrefour\n* Sigma\n* Eurospin\n* MD\n* Margherita\n* A&O\n* Lidl\n* Tuodì\n* Penny Market\n* In’s Mercato\n* DPiù\n* Decò\n* Sogegross\n* Famila\n* Unes\n* Todis\n* Prix Quality\n* Esselunga\n* Pam\n* Rossetto\n* Alì\n* Aldi\n\n[![Candice Criscione Avatar](data:image/svg+xml,%3Csvg%20xmlns='http://www.w3.org/2000/svg'%20viewBox='0%200%2096%2096'%3E%3C/svg%3E)](https://thetuscanmom.com/about-me/)\n\nCandice Criscione\n\nCandice, a mamma of three, is an Italy trip planner and travel guide who has been living, traveling, and working in Italy for over two decades. She uses her extensive experience in Italy to help travelers plan their first (or tenth!) trip to bella Italia. She lives in Tuscany with her husband and three boys and when she’s not writing for Mom in Italy or The Tuscan Mom, you can find her cycling the Tuscan hills and exploring Italy with her boys!\n\n* [Facebook](https://www.facebook.com/MomInItaly/)\n* [Facebook](https://www.facebook.com/ItalyTravelAdvice/)\n* [Pinterest](https://www.pinterest.com/MomInItaly/)\n* [Link](https://flipboard.com/@mominitaly)\n\n## You May Also Like\n\n[![Cypress trees and vineyards decorate the landscape in Tuscany.](data:image/svg+xml,%3Csvg%20xmlns='http://www.w3.org/2000/svg'%20viewBox='0%200%201024%20768'%3E%3C/svg%3E)](https://thetuscanmom.com/trees-in-tuscany/)\n\n### [ICONIC TREES IN TUSCANY + Where to Photograph Them](https://thetuscanmom.com/trees-in-tuscany/)\n\n[![Large Christmas tree next to a stone well in an Italian piazza. Christmas market stall on left.](data:image/svg+xml,%3Csvg%20xmlns='http://www.w3.org/2000/svg'%20viewBox='0%200%201024%20768'%3E%3C/svg%3E)](https://thetuscanmom.com/christmas-in-tuscany/)\n\n### [CHRISTMAS IN TUSCANY – Our 8 Favorite Festive Things to Do](https://thetuscanmom.com/christmas-in-tuscany/)\n\n[![Gigantic tooth statue with a muscular man statue on top of it next to the bell tower in Pietrasanta, Tuscany.](data:image/svg+xml,%3Csvg%20xmlns='http://www.w3.org/2000/svg'%20viewBox='0%200%201024%20768'%3E%3C/svg%3E)](https://thetuscanmom.com/pietrasanta-half-day-itinerary/)\n\n### [PIETRASANTA HALF-DAY ITINERARY – Art, Views, & Food!](https://thetuscanmom.com/pietrasanta-half-day-itinerary/)\n\n ",

Resources](/expatriates/)\n\n\n\n# The Cost of Living in Italy\n\nWritten by [Beatriz Camacho](https://www.internationalinsurance.com/author/beatriz-camacho/)\n\nUpdated on |16 min read\n\nBeatriz Camacho\n\nBeatriz Camacho is an experienced content editor, writer, and literary translator originally from Brazil and currently based in Italy. She specializes in lifestyle and travel content, drawing on life in both countries, while translating Italian literature.\n\n[View Full Bio](https://www.internationalinsurance.com/author/beatriz-camacho/)\n\n[Get a Quote](/quote/) [Compare Plans](/compare/global-medical/)\n\n## Key Takeaways\n\n* Italy has the 34th-highest cost of living in the world on Numbeo's March 2026 Cost of Living Index, while the United States ranks 23rd.\n* Average rents in the United States are around 90% higher than in Italy.\n* A single person renting a one-bedroom apartment in Italy spends $1,800–$2,610 a month on housing, food, transport and utilities in Milan, $1,500–$2,140 in Florence and $1,215–$1,925 in Naples.\n* Italy's average annual full-time adjusted salary was €33,523, below the European Union average of €39,800, according to Eurostat.\n\nWorld-renowned cuisine, excellent healthcare, and a relaxed lifestyle are just some of the reasons expats choose Italy as their new home. Whether you’re moving there for work, retirement, or simply to begin a new chapter abroad, understanding the **cost of living in Italy** is essential before deciding where to settle. This guide breaks down typical expenses in 2026, including housing, utilities, groceries, transportation, and leisure, so you can get a clear picture of what it costs to enjoy *la dolce vita*.\n\n![](https://www.internationalinsurance.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/02/ICI_initials_logo_color.svg)\n\nMoving to Italy? Get Covered\n\nPlanning your move to Italy? Compare personalized international health insurance options that match your destination, budget, and coverage needs.\n\n✔️ Personalized quotes ✔️ Expert guidance ✔️ No obligation\n\n[Get a Quote](/quote/)   [Compare Plans](https://www.internationalinsurance.com/compare/global-medical/)\n\n## Is It Expensive to Live in Italy?\n\nThe cost of living in Italy is slightly below the European average, despite it having the third-largest economy in the European Union. When it comes to the [most expensive countries to live in](https://www.internationalinsurance.com/living-abroad/most-expensive-countries/), Italy ranks 15th in Europe. It has the 34th-highest cost of living in the world, which is a great position given the quality of life it offers.\n\nOn top of that, the country also ranks 25th on the *[OECD Better Life Index](https://www.oecd.org/en/data/tools/well-being-data-monitor/better-life-index.html)*. The OECD Index rates nations based on their population’s general well-being, work-life balance, life satisfaction, and education. That said, one very important factor to consider when discussing the cost of living is the country’s average salary.\n\nItaly doesn’t have a legally established minimum wage. According to the latest [Eurostat data](https://ec.europa.eu/eurostat/web/products-eurostat-news/w/ddn-20251112-1?), the average annual full-time adjusted salary was €33,523 (about $39,371 USD). This was below the EU average of €39,800, placing Italy among the lowest in Western Europe in terms of average earnings.\n\nSo, if you’re wondering “is it expensive to live in Italy?”, the answer is: it depends. To better understand affordability in the country, let’s compare the cost of living in Italy with that in the United States.\n\n## Is Italy Cheaper Than the U.S.?\n\nYes, Italy is generally more affordable than the United States across most major spending categories. According to Numbeo’s *Cost of Living Index* (CLI) in March 2026, the United States ranks higher, at 23rd globally, while Italy ranks 34th. The index compares key expenses, including housing, groceries, transportation, and everyday services.\n\n### Housing Costs in Italy vs. the U.S.\n\nOverall, daily expenses in the U.S. (excluding rent) are about 10.7% higher than in Italy. When rent is included, the gap increases significantly, with the cost of living in the U.S. around 27.4% higher.\n\nRent is perhaps the starkest difference between the two countries, with prices in the U.S. running 90.3% higher, nearly double what you’d pay in Italy. To put that into perspective, the average rent for a one-bedroom apartment outside an Italian city center is $677, compared to $1,360 in the U.S. For a three-bedroom apartment in a city center, Italians pay about $1,515, while Americans spend around $2,690.\n\n### Food Costs in Italy vs. the U.S.\n\nWhen it comes to food, prices in Italy are lower than what you will find in the U.S. Groceries are 15.7% cheaper in Italy, and dining at an Italian restaurant is 13.7% less expensive than eating at a restaurant in America. On the other hand, some everyday expenses are actually cheaper in the U.S. Americans enjoy purchasing power that is 72.2% higher than in Italy, meaning their money goes further in everyday life.\n\nBasic utilities for an 85-square-meter apartment, for example, cost about 10.5% less in the U.S., and gasoline is a significant 57.3% cheaper than in Italy.\n\n## The Most Expensive and Cheapest Italian Cities\n\nWhere you choose to live has a major impact on your monthly budget. While Italy is generally more affordable than the U.S., living costs can vary considerably between cities.\n\nTo better understand how much it costs to live in different parts of the country, here’s a closer look at the cities where expenses are highest and lowest, and what expats can expect to pay.\n\n### The Most Expensive Cities in Italy\n\nWhen it comes to the overall cost of living in Italy – including rent, food, transport, services, and leisure – three cities consistently rank at the top: Milan, Rome, and Florence. However, if you exclude rent, the picture shifts slightly: Milan still ranks highest, followed by Venice and Bologna. This highlights how much housing costs influence overall affordability in major Italian cities.\n\nIn general, the most expensive cities tend to be those with strong tourism, international business activity, or high student populations, all of which drive up demand for everyday goods and services.\n\n#### Milan\n\nMilan remains Italy’s most expensive major city for everyday expenses, excluding rent. Daily life here comes at a premium, with higher costs for groceries, dining out, transportation, and personal services than the national average.\n\nAs the country’s financial and fashion capital, Milan attracts a large international workforce, which also contributes to higher pricing across most categories. Despite the cost, the city offers strong salaries, excellent infrastructure, and a fast-paced urban lifestyle that appeals to professionals.\n\n#### Venice\n\nVenice ranks second among Italy’s most expensive cities, with a single person typically spending around $1,260 per month, excluding rent. Its unique geography plays a major role in pricing. Because the city is built on canals, nearly all goods and services must be transported by boat, which increases operating costs and, ultimately, consumer prices.\n\nEven everyday essentials and dining tend to cost more than in most other Italian cities. However, in return, residents get to live in one of the most distinctive and historically rich urban environments in the world.\n\n#### Bologna\n\nBologna completes the top three, with monthly costs for a single person averaging around $1,130, excluding rent. Known for its strong university presence and renowned food culture, Bologna offers a lively yet manageable urban lifestyle.\n\nWhile it is more affordable than Milan or Venice, demand from students and professionals keeps prices relatively high compared to those in smaller Italian cities. Its central location, walkability, and cultural vibrancy make it one of Italy’s most desirable mid-sized cities, which is reflected in its cost of living.\n\n![](https://www.internationalinsurance.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/02/ICI_initials_logo_color.svg)\n\nFind Health Insurance That Fits Your Budget Abroad\n\nWhether you choose a major city or a more rural destination, international health insurance can help protect you from unexpected medical costs while living in Italy.\n\n[Get a Quote](/quote/)   [Compare Plans](https://www.internationalinsurance.com/compare/global-medical/)\n\n### The Cheapest Cities in Italy\n\nThe cheapest cities in Italy, even when rent is included, are mostly located in the south of the country. These areas tend to have lower housing costs, more affordable daily expenses, and a slower pace of life compared to northern and central Italy.\n\n#### Palermo\n\nPalermo ranks as the most affordable major city in Italy, with a single person typically spending around $940 per month, excluding rent. Everyday life is noticeably cheaper than in northern cities, especially for food, transport, and local services, making it one of the best-value urban areas in the country.\n\nAs the capital of Sicily, it offers a warm Mediterranean climate, lively street markets, and a strong cultural identity shaped by centuries of history.\n\n#### Naples\n\nNaples is also one of the most affordable major cities in Italy, with daily costs estimated to be significantly lower than in northern cities such as Milan and Florence. According to Numbeo, it is also about 40% less expensive than New York on a day-to-day basis.\n\nIt’s a city full of energy, history, and character, where life spills into the streets through markets, cafés, and coastal views. Known for its world-famous pizza and strong local culture, Naples combines affordability with a vibrant urban lifestyle. While it can feel chaotic at times, many international residents are drawn to its authenticity and low cost of living.\n\n#### Turin\n\nTurin stands out as a more affordable option in northern Italy, with monthly costs for a single person averaging around $1,020, excluding rent. Despite being a major industrial and business hub, it offers a more structured and relaxed lifestyle than cities like Milan.\n\nWith strong public transport, respected universities, and growing opportunities in the tech and automotive industries, Turin combines affordability with a high quality of life, especially for professionals and students.\n\n## The Average Cost of Living in Italy\n\nThe cost of living in Italy varies depending on [where you choose to live](https://www.internationalinsurance.com/countries/italy/best-places-to-live/). Smaller towns and rural areas tend to be far cheaper, especially in the south, where housing and everyday expenses are lower. In these areas, you’ll also find a slower pace of life, strong local communities, and a more traditional Italian lifestyle, often at a much lower cost than in major cities.\n\nMid-sized cities offer a balance between affordability and convenience, with access to good services, transport, and cultural activities without the high price tags of larger urban centers. Conversely, international hubs such as Milan, Rome, Florence, and Bologna are the most expensive places to live. This is driven largely by housing demand. Even so, they are still often more affordable than comparable major cities in the United States.\n\nOverall, Italy offers a wide range of cost-of-living options, allowing you to adjust your lifestyle to your budget and location. Here’s a breakdown of the main monthly expenses across the country.\n\n### Rent Prices and Housing Costs in Italy\n\nHousing is the biggest factor influencing Italy’s cost of living. [Italian house prices](https://tg24.sky.it/economia/2025/09/16/prezzi-affitti-bonus-citta?card=3) vary widely depending on the neighborhood (city center vs. outskirts), the property’s condition, and whether the apartment is furnished (furnished units typically cost 10–20% more).\n\nMajor cities like Rome and Milan are by far the most expensive. In Rome, a one-bedroom averages about $1,650 per month, while a two-bedroom averages about $2,200 per month. Milan is similar, with one-bedrooms costing $1,770 and two-bedrooms around $2,275.\n\n#### Regional Differences in Housing Costs\n\nNorthern Italy is generally the most expensive region overall. You can expect to pay around $1,100 per month for a one-bedroom and over $1,445 for a two-bedroom.\n\nCentral Italy is slightly more affordable, with one-bedroom units averaging around $980 and two-bedroom units reaching up to $1,220. Florence is a notable exception, as high tourist demand and short-term rentals drive prices up significantly, with one-bedrooms averaging $1,790 and two-bedrooms around $2,180. Moving to nearby towns can significantly reduce costs.\n\nSouthern Italy offers the best value for money, with a two-bedroom apartment often costing as much as a studio in northern cities. In places like Bari, one-bedroom apartments can range from $580–$810 per month, while in Catania or Palermo, comfortable two-bedroom apartments are typically available for $690–$925 per month.\n\n### Utilities, Internet, and Household Costs\n\nUtilities in Italy can add up quickly, so it’s important to plan ahead. The country has some of the highest electricity prices in Europe.\n\nHere’s a rough idea of monthly costs:\n\n* **45-square-meter apartment (1-2 people)**: $45–70/month, rising to $80–125 in summer or winter when you use A/C or electric heating\n* **85-square-meter apartment (2–3 people)**: $80–110/month, rising to $125–185 during peak seasons\n\nThe average price for household gas is around $1.05–$1.30 per cubic meter (including taxes and fees). Bills typically increase significantly in winter due to heating:\n\n* **45-square-meter apartment:** $60–80/month on average, up to $115–150 in winter\n* **85-square-meter apartment:** $90–125/month on average, up to $175–255 in winter\n\nWater is relatively affordable:\n\n* **1 person:** roughly $15–25/month\n* **Family of 3:** around $40–45/month\n\n#### Other Household Expenses in Italy\n\nOne cost that often surprises foreigners is the [TARI (Tassa sui Rifiuti)](https://www.confcommercio.it/-/tari-tassa-sui-rifiuti), a yearly tax for waste collection and disposal. The amount depends on your city, apartment size, and number of occupants, but generally ranges from $115 to $175 per person per year. Whether you rent or own, you are responsible for paying it.\n\nCompared to the rest of Europe and the United States, internet and mobile plans in Italy are quite affordable. You can expect to pay around $14 per month for a mobile plan and up to $36 per month for home internet.\n\n**Wash on Weekends!** Electricity rates in Italy are often lower during off-peak hours, such as at night and on weekends. Save money on your utility bills by running high-energy appliances like washing machines and dishwashers during these times.\n\n### Food and Groceries\n\nCompared to other countries, local food can be very affordable in Italy if you follow a few smart tips and eat like a local. And no, that doesn’t mean surviving on pasta and pizza. Italians take food quality seriously and typically buy fresh, seasonal produce, ideally sourced locally. This habit not only supports a healthier lifestyle but also helps keep grocery costs down. It’s also one of the best parts of [living in Italy](https://www.internationalinsurance.com/countries/italy/living-in-italy/).\n\nIf you’d rather cook at home, you’ll find it a very budget-friendly option, as shown in the table\\* below:\n\n|  |  |  |  |\n| --- | --- | --- | --- |\n| Expense | **Milan** | **Florence** | **Naples** |\n| Eggs (12, large size) | $5.17 | $4.74 | $4.43 |\n| Milk (1 Liter) | $1.72 | $1.89 | $1.90 |\n| Chicken fillets (1 lb) | $6.62 | $5.67 | $5.37 |\n| Domestic Beer (16.9 oz bottle) | $2.08 | $2.09 | $2.52 |\n| White rice (1 lb) | $1.57 | $1.39 | $1.47 |\n| Local cheese (1 lb) | $10.02 | $11.89 | $7.23 |\n| Bottle of wine (mid-range) | $9.29 | $8.13 | $8.13 |\n\n\\**Prices were sourced from Numbeo and Codacons (an Italian consumer rights organization*)*.*\n\n**Use Supermarket Loyalty Cards!** Most major Italian supermarket chains offer free loyalty cards with weekly deals and discounts that can add up to real savings over the month, so it’s definitely worth getting one. You can sign up online or ask at the store.\n\n![](https://www.internationalinsurance.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/02/ICI_initials_logo_color.svg)\n\nBuild Healthcare Into Your Cost of Living Plan\n\nYour rent, food, and transportation matter, but so does access to quality healthcare. Compare international health plans designed for expats living in Italy.\n\n[Get a Quote](/quote/)   [Compare Plans](https://www.internationalinsurance.com/compare/global-medical/)\n\n### Transportation Costs in Italy\n\nFuel in Italy costs almost twice as much as in the U.S., though it remains slightly below the European average. The good news is that Italian cars tend to be smaller and more fuel-efficient than American ones.\n\nHere’s a quick look at [current fuel prices in Italy](https://lab24.ilsole24ore.com/prezzo-benzina/):\n\n* **Diesel:** approximately $2.40-$2.55 per liter\n* **Gasoline** **(petrol):** approximately $2.10-$2.25 per liter\n* **LPG:** approximately $0.94 per liter\n* **Natural gas (methane/CNG):** approximately $1.75-$1.85 per liter\n\nKeep in mind that prices vary by region and are usually higher at highway service stations.\n\n#### The Cost of Owning a Car in Italy\n\nBeyond fuel, owning a car in Italy comes with additional costs that average around $335 per month, not including vehicle depreciation, a significant hidden expense.\n\nHere’s an estimated annual breakdown:\n\n* **Insurance:** $600-700\n* **Road tax:** $235-470\n* **Maintenance:** $180-940\n* **Fuel:** $1,770-2,360\n\nIf you live in a major city, public transportation is a strong alternative. Buses, metro, and trams make it easy to get around without worrying about parking or traffic. A monthly pass typically costs $37–$45, and a single ticket costs about $2.\n\nMost transit companies offer monthly and annual passes, along with discounts for students, workers, and people over 65, so it’s worth checking what’s available in your city.\n\n### Healthcare and Insurance\n\nItaly has one of the best healthcare systems in the world. Foreign residents who meet certain requirements may be eligible to enroll in [Italy’s public healthcare service](https://www.internationalinsurance.com/countries/italy/healthcare/) (*Servizio Sanitario Nazionale,* or SSN). This covers basic care at little to no cost, including GP visits, emergency care, hospital stays, and some vaccinations.\n\nOther services, such as specialist appointments and lab tests, require a small co-payment (called a *ticket sanitario*), though exemptions may apply based on age, income, and other factors.\n\nWait times for specialists can vary by region, which is where private [health insurance in Italy](https://www.internationalinsurance.com/countries/italy/health-insurance/) comes in. Private coverage provides faster access to appointments, diagnostics, and private clinics, and helps cover costs not included in the public system.\n\nBelow is an overview of typical [private healthcare costs in Italy](https://cup24.it/prezzi.html):\n\n|  |  |\n| --- | --- |\n| Service | **Estimated Cost** |\n| Cardiologist | $125 |\n| Gynecologist/Urologist | $140 |\n| Physiotherapy (per session) | $60-$110 |\n| Basic check-up (lab tests) | $95-$175 |\n| Ultrasound | $85-$120 |\n| MRI | $160-$470 |\n\n![](https://www.internationalinsurance.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/02/ICI_initials_logo_color.svg)\n\nWhy Many Expats Choose Private Insurance in Italy\n\nWhile Italy offers a public healthcare system, many expats choose private health insurance to access private hospitals, reduce wait times, and avoid unexpected out-of-pocket medical costs.\n\n[Get a Quote](/quote/)   [Compare Plans](https://www.internationalinsurance.com/compare/global-medical/)\n\n### Entertainment and Lifestyle\n\nItalians place great value on leisure and socializing, especially when it involves food, sports, and cultural activities. That’s why it’s important to include these costs in your budget so you can fully enjoy the Italian lifestyle.\n\nCosts vary by region and whether you’re in a touristy area, but here are some average prices:\n\n|  |  |\n| --- | --- |\n| Activity | **Estimated Cost** |\n| Cinema | $12 |\n| Theater | $20-$70 |\n| Lunch menu | $19 |\n| Dinner for two in a mid-range restaurant | $82 |\n| Domestic beer in a pub (16 oz.) | $5.80 |\n| Fast food meal | $12 |\n| Aperitivo (1 drink and snacks) | $12 |\n| Cappuccino | $2 |\n| Gym membership (1 month) | $57 |\n| Tennis court booking (1 hr on the weekend) | $27 |\n\nItaly also offers many free or low-cost activities, including open-air exhibitions, concerts, street festivals, and food fairs throughout the year.\n\n**Enjoy Free Museums!** Many museums are free on the first Sunday of each month through the *[Domenica al Museo](https://musei.cultura.gov.it/en/eventi/domenicalmuseo)* (Sunday at the museum). It’s a great opportunity to explore world-class art, history, and culture without spending a euro.\n\n### Childcare and Education\n\nEducation costs in Italy vary widely depending on family income and region. The public school system is generally free from age three, as it is government-subsidized. However, it’s important to account for “hidden” costs such as meals, books, and transportation, especially in primary and secondary school.\n\n#### Daycare (Ages 0–3)\n\nPublic daycare is not free, and fees are set by each municipality based on the [ISEE](https://www.inps.it/it/it/dettaglio-scheda.it.schede-servizio-strumento.schede-servizi.Portale-unico-ISEE.html). The ISEE is Italy’s official measure of household income and assets used to determine eligibility for public services.\n\nIn northern cities, full-time public daycare can cost around $660 per month at full price. However, the government offers a *Bonus Asilo Nido* to help reduce costs for both public and approved private daycare providers, based on income. In practice, a middle-income family can expect to pay between $95 and $355 per month after discounts. Private daycare is typically more expensive, even with the bonus applied.\n\n#### Primary and Secondary School (Ages 6+)\n\nPublic schools do not charge tuition or meals at this stage, but other costs apply, including textbooks, school supplies, field trips, meals (paid separately), and school transport if needed. A key benefit is that these expenses are tax-deductible at 19% up to €1,000 per child, providing up to €190 ($224) back per child.\n\nPrivate school fees increased notably in 2024. Here’s a general breakdown:\n\n* **Primary (ages 6–11):** $2,950–$14,150/year\n* **Lower secondary (ages 11–14):** $3,540–$9,440/year\n* **Upper secondary + High school (ages 14–19):** $4,720–$11,795/year\n* **International schools:** often above $11,795, and can reach $29,490/year\n\n## Monthly Budget for Living in Italy\n\nKnowing your everyday expenses makes budgeting for life in Italy much easier. To help you, this section compares typical costs across Milan (North), Florence (Central), and Naples (South).\n\nThe table below shows the average monthly expenses for a single person renting a one-bedroom apartment in three of the country’s most popular cities:\n\n|  |  |  |  |\n| --- | --- | --- | --- |\n| Expense | **Milan** | **Florence** | **Naples** |\n| Housing | $1,270-$1,670 | $950-$1,285 | $720-$1,155 |\n| Food | $345-$580 | $370-$520 | $325-$460 |\n| Transport | $46-$70 | $40-$70 | $40-$70 |\n| Utilities | $167-$290 | $145-$265 | $130-$240 |\n| **Monthly Total:** | **$1,800-$2,610** | **$1,500-$2,140** | **$1,215-$1,925** |\n\nThe table below shows the average monthly expenses for a family of four living in a three-bedroom apartment in three of Italy’s most popular cities:\n\n|  |  |  |  |\n| --- | --- | --- | --- |\n| Expense | **Milan** | **Florence** | **Naples** |\n| Housing | $2,075-$3,445 | $1,620-$2,420 | $1,260-$2,100 |\n| Food | $920-$1,380 | $800-$1,270 | $690-$1,150 |\n| Transport | $90-$170 | $80-$150 | $70-$140 |\n| Utilities | $290-$470 | $265-$450 | $230-$415 |\n| **Monthly Total:** | **$3,375-$5,465** | **$2,765-$4,290** | **$2,250-$3,800** |\n\n## Planning Your Move to Italy\n\nFrom world-famous cuisine and rich cultural traditions to beautiful landscapes and a relaxed lifestyle, Italy offers an exciting opportunity for expats looking to start a new chapter abroad. While living costs can vary significantly by location, choosing a smaller city or town can often offer a more affordable way to enjoy the Italian lifestyle.\n\nBefore relocating, it’s important to create a realistic [budget for moving abroad](https://www.internationalinsurance.com/moving-abroad/budget-for-move-abroad/) that covers more than just everyday expenses. Consider factors such as housing, healthcare, exchange rates, banking fees, and tax obligations to get a complete picture of your financial needs.\n\nItaly’s tax system can be complex, especially for new residents. If you become a tax resident (generally by spending more than 183 days per year in the country), you may be required to report and pay tax on worldwide income. Seeking advice from an [international tax firm](https://www.internationalinsurance.com/finance/expat-tax-services/) before relocating can help you understand your responsibilities and ensure a smoother transition.\n\nWith careful planning and a clear understanding of the costs involved, the country can be an affordable and rewarding place to call home.\n\n![](https://www.internationalinsurance.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/02/ICI_initials_logo_color.svg)\n\nEnjoy Life Abroad With the Right Coverage\n\nFrom everyday expenses to unexpected medical needs, make sure you’re fully prepared. Explore health insurance options that support your lifestyle abroad.\n\n[Get a Quote](/quote/)   [Compare Plans](https://www.internationalinsurance.com/compare/global-medical/)\n\n## Cost of Living in Italy FAQs\n\n* ### \n\n  The cost of living in Italy depends on your location, lifestyle, and housing choices. A single person can expect to spend roughly $1,200–$2,600 USD per month, including rent. More affordable cities such as Naples and Palermo can cost closer to the lower end of this range, while expensive cities like Milan, Rome, and Florence typically require a larger budget due to higher housing and everyday expenses.\n* ### \n\n  Rent prices in Italy vary widely depending on location, apartment size, and whether you live in a city center or outside the main urban areas. A one-bedroom apartment can cost around $580–$925 per month in many southern cities, while prices in major cities like Milan and Rome can exceed $1,600 per month. Smaller towns and areas outside city centers often offer much lower housing costs, making them attractive options for retirees, remote workers, and expats seeking a more affordable lifestyle.\n* ### \n\n  Italy is generally more affordable than many Western European countries. It ranks [15th in Europe for cost of living](https://www.euronews.com/business/2025/07/06/cost-of-living-which-are-the-cheapest-and-most-expensive-countries-in-europe), with prices below countries such as France, Germany, the Netherlands, and the United Kingdom. However, costs vary significantly within the country. Major cities like Milan, Rome, and Florence are among the most expensive places to live, while smaller towns and southern regions offer lower housing and everyday expenses.\n* ### \n\n  The salary needed to live comfortably in Italy depends on your location, lifestyle, and housing costs. A single person typically spends around $1,200–$2,600 USD per month, including rent, depending on the city. Italy’s average full-time salary is around $39,371 USD per year (about $3,280 USD per month before tax), which is below the EU average. Expats with a foreign income, pension, or remote salary may find living costs more affordable, especially in smaller cities and southern regions where housing and everyday expenses are lower.\n* ### \n\n  Expats may need to pay for [healthcare in Italy](https://www.internationalinsurance.com/countries/italy/healthcare/) depending on their residency status and eligibility for the public healthcare system. Those who qualify can register with the *Servizio Sanitario Nazionale* (SSN), which provides access to essential healthcare services at little to no cost. However, some specialist visits and diagnostic tests may require a small co-payment. Many expats also choose private [health insurance in Italy](https://www.internationalinsurance.com/countries/italy/health-insurance/) for faster access to care and additional coverage.\n\n## Related 7+ \n\n- [x] Pleasant 6+  \n\n### Quick facts about Naples\n\n**Naples** is a coastal Italian city known for its **Neapolitan pizza**, **historic centre**, and proximity to **Pompeii** and the **Bay of Naples**. **Agoda** lists diverse accommodations from boutique hotels to family-friendly options and supports planning for peak seasons like **spring (April–June)** and **fall (September–October)**.\n\n### Key Points\n\n*   **Historic sites** include the **National Archaeological Museum**, **Museo Cappella Sansevero**, and **Underground Naples**.\n*   Peak visitation occurs in **July–August** with festivals such as the **Feast of San Gennaro** and **Naples Pizza Village**.\n*   Proximity to **Pompeii** and **Mount Vesuvius** enables popular day trips and drives heritage tourism.\n*   **Public transport** (metro, buses, trams) plus walking and coastal routes facilitate mobility; day passes reduce costs.\n*   **Agoda** aggregates hotel options and deals, influencing booking choices for beachfront, boutique, and budget properties.\n*   Culinary highlights (e.g., **Antica Pizzeria Di Matteo**, **sfogliatella**) and street food underpin gastronomic tourism.\n\nOur My husband and I were at a total loss when we left one of our bags in the cab after check out, when we were dropped off at the train station. The bag contained our passports amongst other things. You could imagine our panic! When we phoned the hotel, the staff who picked up immediately tried to calm us down and then she spent the next 30 mins calling around various cab companies trying to hunt down the cab driver. We thought it was a lost cause but when we called back, she gave us the good news that she had found the driver and had arranged for him to return to the station with our bag! In our rush, we did not get the name of the lady staff who helped us but we would like to thank her from the bottom of our hearts for what she did for us. She could have easily chosen to not to help us, but instead, she pulled off a miracle! All the other staff from reception to housekeeping were most friendly and accommodating during our stay. We also loved our room and how spacious and modern it was. Breakfast was a healthy spread and the view from the terrace is amazing. Location wise, it is not far from all the cool spots Naples has to offer. The staff were friendly and helpful, however the official address makes it somewhat difficult to find if you’re traveling by car, as the Naples traffic is somewhat chaotic. If you use the address Largo Barsanti E. e Matteucci C. this will bring you to the entrance where the parking area is located, as the front entrance is not accessible by car. We ate dinner at the restaurant, however the food was only average and barely warm. Breakfast was fairly typical of Italy with lots of cakes and sweet biscuits, but not a lot of healthy or savory options. The room was large and very clean with a large bathroom. The decor in the rooms was modern but the chairs were unfortunately not very comfortable. The WiFi connection was very poor. The small bar/pizzeria uphhill (100 mtrs) serves good pizza at reasonable prices. Downhill is a Carrefour grocery shop for essentials. Nearby on Piazza Gesu is the stop for the hop-on hop-off tourist bus. The hotel can be difficult to find. Look for Astra Cafe (off street) and press the bell on the right of the large gate. The Europeo is in the student quarter, where there is a lot of graffiti, but don't be put off. It is safe, secure, inexpensive and will gift you many unforgettable moments in a great city. His dad also offers a tour of Pompei and Vesuvio by car for a good price, which...\n\nIt is the most welcoming hostel I've been to. It's very clean and new, and a great atmosphere with really nice and interesting guests. It's a family-run hostel: Alessandro (the owner), and often his mom, cook an authentic Italian dinner every night and his dad makes delicious homemade wine. His dad also offers a tour of Pompei and Vesuvio by car for a good price, which is definitely worthwhile because it's easy to get there and Pompei is amazing. There is staff available 24 hours a day, every day, and everyone is really nice and easy to talk to. They are expanding the place, and starting in the spring there will even be a private pool. There is also a garden outside with fresh citrus fruits to eat and juice. It's clear that the owners care a lot about the hostel and have a lot to offer about Naples. We only have positive things to say about this hotel. 1)Walking distance from Garibaldi train station. 2) Has a lot of cheap pop up markets (just watch out for your things) 3) A lot of delicious and affordable food (there is an Indian restaurant opposite the street with the best briyani and beef curry) 4) A very helpful male maintenance staff who helped us with our charger issue. Imagine the energy of Casa della ...\n\nFabrizio Moro is a name that resonates deeply with music lovers, especially those who appreciate the heart and soul of Italian pop. If you find yourself in Naples, Italy, you’re in for a treat! This vibrant city, with its rich history and stunning architecture, is the perfect backdrop for experiencing Moro's electrifying performances. Imagine the energy of Casa della Musica - Federico I, a venue known for its superb acoustics and intimate atmosphere, where you can feel the music pulsing through your veins. Italy\n\nNaples, Italy, is a city that pulses with life and energy, offering a rich tapestry of culture, history, and charm that beckons travelers from around the globe. Strolling through the historic streets, visitors are greeted by the aroma of freshly baked pizza wafting from local pizzerias and the lively chatter of Neapolitans going about their daily routines. The unique character of Naples is evident in its vibrant street art, bustling markets, and the warm hospitality of its residents. This city is not just a place to visit; it’s an experience that immerses you in the heart of Italian life.\n\nThe artistic heritage of Naples is both profound and captivating. From the iconic works of Caravaggio to the stunning frescoes of the Cappella Sansevero, art lovers will find themselves in a paradise of creativity. The city is also home to numerous museums and galleries that showcase its historical significance, such as the National Archaeological Museum, which houses artifacts from Pompeii and Herculaneum. Whether you’re wandering through the narrow alleys of Spaccanapoli or admiring the grandeur of the Royal Palace, Naples offers a feast for the senses that is sure to leave a lasting impression.\n\nAs you explore Naples, be prepared to be enchanted by its lively atmosphere. The city is famous for its street performers, local musicians, and vibrant festivals that celebrate everything from food to art. The lively piazzas are perfect for people-watching, where you can sip a cappuccino while soaking in the sights and sounds of this dynamic city. With every corner you turn, Naples reveals a new layer of its rich cultural heritage, making it a destination that truly captivates the heart and soul of every traveler.\n\n### When to Experience Naples in All Its Glory\n\nTiming your visit to Naples can greatly enhance your experience in this bustling city. The best times to visit are typically during the spring (April to June) and fall (September to October) when the weather is pleasantly mild, and the tourist crowds are manageable. During these months, you can explore outdoor attractions and historical sites without the sweltering heat of summer or the chill of winter. The vibrant colors of spring flowers and the golden hues of autumn create a picturesque backdrop for your adventures.\n\nSummer months (July and August) bring a lively atmosphere to Naples, with numerous festivals and events taking place. However, be prepared for higher temperatures and increased tourist traffic. If you prefer a more laid-back experience, the winter months (December to February) can be surprisingly charming. While some attractions may have reduced hours, the city takes on a quieter, more intimate feel, allowing for a deeper connection with its history and culture. Plus, you can enjoy the festive holiday decorations that adorn the streets.\n\nRegardless of when you choose to visit, Naples has something to offer year-round. From the bustling Christmas markets to the lively summer street fairs, there is always an event or activity to immerse yourself in. Keep an eye on the local calendar for unique happenings, such as the Feast of San Gennaro in September, which showcases the city’s culinary delights and rich traditions. With a little planning, you can experience Naples in all its glory, no matter the season.\n\n### Essential Travel Tips for Navigating Naples\n\nTraveling to Naples can be an exhilarating experience, but being prepared with some essential tips can make your adventure even more enjoyable. First and foremost, it’s important to keep safety in mind. While Naples is generally safe for tourists, it’s wise to stay vigilant, especially in crowded areas. Keep your belongings secure and be cautious of pickpockets in busy markets and public transport. Familiarizing yourself with local customs, such as greeting people with a friendly *“Buongiorno”* or *“Buonasera,”* can also go a long way in making connections with locals.\n\nLearning a few basic phrases in Italian can enhance your experience and help you navigate the city with ease. Simple phrases like *“Per favore”* (please) and *“Grazie”* (thank you) will be appreciated by the locals and can create a warm atmosphere during your interactions. Additionally, consider downloading translation apps or carrying a small phrasebook to assist you in conversations. Understanding the local dining etiquette, such as not asking for cheese on your seafood pasta, can also help you enjoy authentic culinary experiences.\n\nLastly, it’s advisable to plan your itinerary in advance to make the most out of your time in Naples. With a plethora of attractions, from historical sites to culinary adventures, having a rough outline can help you prioritize what you want to see. Consider purchasing a Naples City Pass for access to popular attractions and transportation discounts. With these tips in hand, you’ll be well-equipped to explore the enchanting streets of Naples with confidence.\n\n### Where to Stay for the Ultimate Naples Experience\n\nFinding the perfect accommodation in Naples is key to enjoying your visit to this vibrant city. From luxury hotels to budget-friendly options, there’s something to suit every traveler’s needs. For those seeking a touch of elegance, the Hotel Royal Continental offers stunning views of the Bay of Naples and a fantastic breakfast spread that guests rave about. This hotel is perfect for those looking to indulge in comfort while being close to major attractions.\n\nIf you're on a budget, the B&B Hotel Napoli is a great choice. This modern hotel is known for its cleanliness and friendly staff, making it a popular option among travelers. Guests appreciate the convenient location, which allows easy access to public transport and local attractions. For families, the Holiday Inn Naples is spacious and comfortable, with fantastic amenities that cater to both adults and children. Reviewers often highlight the fantastic service and the welcoming atmosphere that makes it feel like a home away from home.\n\nFor travelers looking for something unique, boutique hotels in Naples offer a charming experience. Consider the Hotel Eden, which provides a cozy ambiance and personalized service that sets it apart from larger chains. These smaller hotels often reflect the local culture and provide a more intimate experience. No matter your budget or preferences, Naples boasts a wide range of accommodations that ensure a memorable stay in this captivating city.\n\n  \n\n### Why Agoda.com is Your Go-To for Naples Hotel Deals\n\nWhen planning your stay in Naples, choosing the right platform to book your accommodations can make all the difference.
